`docs/ml_report.md`:

```md
# Detecting Click Automation from Server-Observable Timing in Minecraft: A Reproducible Pipeline, an Adaptive-Adversary Evaluation, and What One Real Session Can and Cannot Show

*Alejandro Ramírez Arias — extension of the Bachelor's thesis "Cheating y anti-cheat en
videojuegos: enfoque de ciberdefensa y desarrollo de un detector server-side en Minecraft con
bastionado".*

> **Reading guide.** This report separates two kinds of result:
> - **REAL** results come from the pipeline running on the recorded sessions in `ml/data/raw/`.
>   Today that is one 65-second session from one participant.
> - **DEMO** results come from synthetic humans and synthetic adversaries and are marked
>   *DEMO - synthetic, not evidence about real players*. They show that the method and code
>   work, and how the detectors behave *under the stated assumptions*. They are not
>   measurements of real players.
>
> Every number below was produced by `ml/run_all.ps1`; the source tables are in `ml/results/`.

## Abstract

Server-side anti-cheat systems often detect autoclickers with a fixed rule: an inter-click
interval below 1000/15 ms is a violation. We extend a Paper 1.18.2 anti-cheat plugin with a
reproducible pipeline that records labelled sessions, cleans the event stream, extracts
window-level timing features, and evaluates threshold, unsupervised, supervised and sequence
models. Thresholds are calibrated on held-out humans and data are split by participant. Recall is
reported at fixed false-positive rates.

**REAL.** The first real session (one participant, 65 s) yields three observations:
- one physical click produces up to three server events, so the deployed detector measured the
  wrong quantity;
- held-button mining generates one swing per tick, so a naive switch to swing timing would flag
  56 of 76 intervals of legitimate play;
- intervals cluster on the 50 ms tick grid (73% of active intervals within ±5 ms, against 20%
  without quantization; n = 15, KS p = 0.014). A fixed-rate clicker is therefore *not* observed
  as zero-variance.

No real window survives cleaning, so no real detection metric can be reported yet.

**DEMO.** On synthetic data with placeholder humans:
- gradient-boosted trees reach recall 0.984 at a realised FPR of 0.8%, where the original rule
  flags 86% of synthetic human windows;
- an adversary that resamples the human interval distribution is nearly undetectable from
  timing alone (recall ≤ 0.20);
- an evolution-strategy adversary optimised against XGBoost drives its recall to 0.008, but is
  caught by other detectors;
- after adversarial training, a re-optimised adversary still evades half the windows;
- simulated lag raises the FPR of several models.

The trained model is exported to ONNX and integrated in the plugin off the main thread, behind a
fail-safe switch that defaults to the original detector.

## 1. Introduction and threat model

The thesis treats cheating as a defensive-security problem under a **man-at-the-end (MATE)**
threat model. The player controls the client, its memory and its input devices, so the server
can only judge the *observable consequences* of client behaviour [Collins et al. 2024;
Alangari & Alharbi 2025]. Click automation (autoclickers, macros) is a natural target because it
leaves a temporal trace: the sequence of attack swings the server receives. The thesis detector
flags any interval shorter than 66 ms (15 CPS) and accumulates violation levels (VL) before a
kick. Section 2.3 of the thesis already anticipates the weakness: an attacker can add jitter to
look human, while more complex models cost transparency.

This extension asks: **can a statistical/ML model detect click automation, with and without
jitter, from server-observable timing where fixed thresholds fail? How robust is it against an
adaptive adversary and against network lag?** The thesis priorities are kept:
- false positives are the costliest error;
- decisions must be explainable and auditable;
- the detector must not degrade server TPS;
- the extension must not weaken the existing hardening (RBAC, fail-safe configuration,
  auditing, asynchronous logging).

**Adversary capabilities assumed here.** The attacker can generate any sequence of click times
at the client: fixed rate, jittered, resampled from recorded human play, or optimised against a
known detector (white-box, adaptive). The attacker cannot change how the server timestamps
events, nor the client's 20 Hz tick, unless they modify the client. A modified client could send
swing packets at arbitrary times; this is noted as a limitation in Section 7. Out of scope:
perception cheats, and anything requiring client instrumentation.

## 2. Related work

- **Defence families.** The thesis reviews server-side, client-side and kernel-level anti-cheat.
  Alangari and Alharbi [2025] organise technical defences into server-side detection,
  client-side anti-tamper, kernel drivers and hardware-assisted TEEs. They compare them on
  detection effectiveness, overhead, privacy and scalability, and stress that server-side
  methods are less intrusive but see less of the client.
- **The attacker side.** Collins et al. [2024] study the cheat market and the sturdiness of
  client-side anti-cheat across 11 shooters. They find cheat prices track defensive sturdiness,
  which is the economic argument behind "raise the attacker's cost" used in the thesis.
- **Server-side ML.** Closest in spirit is HAWK [Zhang et al. 2024/2026], a server-side ML
  anti-cheat for CS:GO. It imitates human expert review with multi-view features and is
  evaluated on large real datasets; its emphasis on real-world data and expert-like explanations
  is exactly what this extension still lacks. Transformer-based detection from game telemetry
  has also been proposed (AntiCheatPT, arXiv:2508.06348).
- **Methods used.** Isolation Forest [Liu et al. 2008], One-Class SVM [Schölkopf et al. 2001],
  Random Forest [Breiman 2001], XGBoost [Chen & Guestrin 2016], and SHAP / TreeSHAP for
  explanations [Lundberg & Lee 2017; Lundberg et al. 2020].


## 3. Data and cleaning

### 3.1 Recording

`SessionRecorder` (`/ciberplugin record start <player> <label> <participant>`) writes labelled
sessions asynchronously through a bounded queue that counts dropped events (backpressure). It
records click events (`swing`, `interact_air`, `interact_block`), movement deltas with
on-ground, ping and TPS, block breaks, and a session summary.

### 3.2 REAL findings that shaped the design

The only real session is P01: 65 s, legacy label `human` → `human_unspecified`, 111 click events,
649 moves, 24 breaks, 0 dropped.

1. **One click, several events.** 34 of 110 raw gaps between click events are below 15 ms. A
   single press can produce `interact_block` (dig start), the `break`, `interact_air` (the
   server-side ray trace for the swing) and the `swing`. The deployed detector measured
   intervals between `interact_*` events, which mixes these.
2. **Held button = one swing per tick.** While the button is held the client sends a swing every
   tick (~50 ms). The real data show the `break` arriving *before* those swings when blocks break
   instantly. A held-button state machine handles this: a dig marker opens a run that lasts while
   swings stay ≤ 65 ms apart. Here 56 of 77 accepted swings are held.
3. **Quantization.** Intervals cluster on multiples of 50 ms (Figure 1).

   | intervals | n | within ±5 ms of the grid | KS vs uniform residuals |
   |---|---|---|---|
   | all (held + active) | 71 | 74.6% | p = 1.4e-8 |
   | active (real clicks) | 15 | 73.3% | p = 0.014 |

   Without quantization the expected share is 20%. This is consistent with the client handling
   mouse input once per 20 Hz client tick. The residual spread (MAD-based single-event
   σ ≈ 2.5 ms) is used to calibrate the jitter of every synthetic generator.
4. **Movement.** Moves start ~12 s after `/record start`. The spawn trim is therefore applied
   relative to the first move.
5. **Game mode.** All 24 breaks arrive ≤ 5 ms after the dig start, and there is sustained upward
   motion. Both suggest creative mode, a confound for every movement and break rule.

![Figure 1](../ml/results/figures/real_tick_quantization.png)
*Figure 1 (REAL). Accepted swing intervals and their residual to the 50 ms grid.*

### 3.3 Original rules on the real session (REAL, anecdotal)

| rule (thesis Annex I) | stream | violations |
|---|---|---|
| AutoClicker 0 < Δ < 66 ms | interact events, as deployed | 1 / 33 |
| | all raw events | 59 / 110 |
| | swings, de-duplicated, not held-filtered | 56 / 76 |
| | **cleaned** (held excluded) | **0 / 20** |
| FastBreak < 450 ms | break intervals | 15 / 23 |
| Speed > 0.5 (effective: `0.1` clamped to 0.5) | trimmed moves | 70 / 608 |
| Speed > 1.2 (thesis text) | trimmed moves | 0 / 608 |
| Fly: airborne and \|dy\| > 1 | trimmed moves | 7 / 608 (a fall) |

The session is human, so every violation is a false positive. With n = 1 these are
illustrations of failure modes, not rates.

## 4. Method

### 4.1 Windows and features

- **Windows:** 50 accepted swings with stride 25 (main), and 5 s with stride 2.5 s.
- **Interval selection:** for every window, the intervals ending in a non-held swing are kept.
  Pauses > 1 s are counted separately; a window needs ≥ 10 remaining intervals.
- **Features:** mean, std, coefficient of variation, skewness and excess kurtosis (biased
  moments), min, p05, p95, Shannon entropy of a fixed 25 ms histogram, lag-1 autocorrelation,
  maximum clicks in any 1 s, fraction of intervals within ±5 ms of a 50 ms multiple, duplicate
  fraction (swings merged by de-duplication), and pause fraction.
- **Routing:** windows with ≥ 50% held swings go to "mining" and are not scored.
- **Audit-only fields:** `held_frac`, ping and TPS are logged but deliberately **not** model
  inputs. As inputs they would create cheap evasion paths and a dependence on network
  conditions.
- **Exact port:** the definitions are written out explicitly so that the Java plugin
  reproduces them exactly. JUnit tests compare Java and Python on fixtures with tolerance 1e-6.

### 4.2 Detectors

1. **Original rule** (fixed, uncalibrated): a window is flagged if any interval has
   0 < Δ < 66 ms. It is applied to raw interact events and to cleaned intervals.
2. **cv_rule:** one explainable statistic with a calibrated threshold.
3. **Unsupervised, humans only:** Isolation Forest (300 trees), One-Class SVM (RBF, ν = 0.05).
4. **Supervised:** Random Forest (300 trees), XGBoost (300 trees, depth 4).
5. **Sequence model:** a 1D-CNN (two conv layers) on log-scaled interval sequences.

### 4.3 Evaluation protocol

- **Split:** StratifiedGroupKFold (5 folds) by **participant**.
- **Threshold placement:** inside each training fold, 25% of human participants are held out
  and used *only* to place thresholds at FPR targets of 1% and 0.1%. The test fold never
  influences fitting or thresholds.
- **Main metric:** recall at fixed FPR, with the realised test FPR reported alongside.
- **Other metrics:** PR-AUC, F1 and confusion counts at 1%, and a session-level alarm rate
  (≥ 3 flagged windows, mimicking VL accumulation).
- **Uncertainty:** 95% bootstrap intervals resample participants within class (1,000
  replicates).
- **Real data:** with one participant, leave-one-participant-out is impossible. The code runs
  the same protocol on real windows when ≥ 2 groups per class exist, and falls back to session
  grouping with an explicit warning.

### 4.4 Synthetic data (DEMO)

- **Humans:** 40 synthetic participants, each with 3 × 180 s combat sessions and 1 × 90 s
  held-button mining session.
  - Combat intervals come from a lognormal generator with AR(1) drift, geometric bouts, pauses
    and slow fatigue.
  - Hyper-parameters are **placeholders** (median 90–170 ms, log-sd 0.20–0.45, AR 0.1–0.5),
    because the real data contain 15 active intervals and the fitting routine requires ≥ 300.
    It switches automatically when that is reached.
- **Adversaries:** 24 synthetic participants per level × 3 sessions:
  - `none`: fixed 8–20 CPS;
  - `uniform`: ±10–50% jitter;
  - `gaussian`: 5–30% sd;
  - `human_like`: i.i.d. lognormal draws from the human hyper-distribution, i.e. matching
    marginals without temporal structure;
  - `optimized`: see Section 5.2.
  - All levels share the same bout structure.
- **Observation model:** all generators pass through the same model. Client-tick quantization
  (random phase, clicks in the same tick sent back-to-back) comes first, then Gaussian
  per-packet jitter (σ = 2.5 ms, from the real data), with TCP order preserved.

## 5. Experiments and results

### 5.1 Detection of standard autoclickers (DEMO)

50-click windows: 4,406 human and 16,902 attack windows, 40 human and 72 attacker participants.
Brackets are 95% participant-bootstrap CIs.

| detector | recall@1% | realised FPR | recall@0.1% | PR-AUC |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| original rule, raw events | 0.952 | 0.859 [0.791, 0.921] | – | – |
| original rule, cleaned | 0.952 | 0.859 | – | – |
| cv_rule | 0.286 [0.198, 0.370] | 0.014 | 0.195 | 0.978 |
| Isolation Forest | 0.741 [0.664, 0.813] | 0.022 | 0.360 | 0.995 |
| One-Class SVM | 0.916 [0.866, 0.959] | 0.016 | 0.607 | 0.997 |
| Random Forest | 0.986 [0.970, 0.998] | 0.009 | 0.700 | 1.000 |
| XGBoost | 0.984 [0.969, 0.996] | 0.008 | 0.921 [0.880, 0.956] | 1.000 |
| 1D-CNN | 0.834 [0.768, 0.891] | 0.020 | 0.764 | 0.993 |

**Reading this table honestly.**
- **The 86% FPR of the original rule** follows from quantization plus the *assumed* lower tail
  of placeholder humans: any true interval below ~75 ms is observed as 50 ms. Whether real fast
  clickers behave like this is unknown, and the one real session had 0 cleaned violations.
- **The 0.1% operating point** relies on ~950 calibration windows, so its threshold is close to
  the maximum human score seen. Realised FPRs at that target (0.1–0.4%) should be read as
  "≲ 0.5%".
- **5-second windows** give similar ranking. Session alarms are higher with them (XGBoost:
  16.7% of human sessions with ≥ 3 flagged windows vs 2.5% with 50-click windows), because
  overlapping short windows give more chances to fire.
- **What drives detection.** SHAP (Section 5.4) shows supervised models rely on the *shape* of
  the tick-count distribution: histogram entropy, mean, lag-1 autocorrelation. A fixed 12 CPS
  clicker produces a deterministic 50/100 ms pattern with strongly negative autocorrelation and
  low entropy.

### 5.2 Adaptive adversary and adversarial training (DEMO)

**Round 0.** Detectors are trained on humans + `none` only. The `optimized` adversary is a
(μ = 4, λ = 12) evolution strategy over (median, spread, AR coefficient, pause probability,
pause length), minimising the mean round-0 XGBoost score subject to ≥ 12 CPS within bouts. It
converged to a 40 ms median with large spread and frequent pauses, at 12.5 CPS.

**Round 1.** Adversarial training adds `uniform`, `gaussian`, `human_like` and `optimized`
participants. The new model is attacked again by a fresh optimisation, `optimized_r2`, which
converged to a 71 ms median, spread 0.30, AR 0.58, at 12.1 CPS.

Recall at 1% FPR:

| model | none | uniform | gaussian | human_like | optimized | optimized_r2 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| XGBoost, round 0 | 1.000 | 0.908 | 0.960 | 0.059 | **0.008** | – |
| XGBoost, round 1 | 0.998 | 0.956 | 0.978 | 0.197 | 0.990 | **0.493** |
| Random Forest, round 1 | 0.993 | 0.963 | 0.973 | 0.162 | 0.930 | 0.329 |
| One-Class SVM (humans only) | 0.913 | 0.779 | 0.780 | 0.018 | 1.000 | 0.509 |
| original rule (FPR 86%) | 0.890 | 0.999 | 0.966 | 0.824 | 1.000 | 1.000 |

![Figure 2](../ml/results/figures/adversarial_recall_vs_level.png)
*Figure 2 (DEMO). Recall vs adversary level with participant-bootstrap CIs, before and after
adversarial training. Legends show each detector's realised human FPR.*

**Findings (DEMO).**
1. **Timing-only detection cannot separate a clicker that reproduces the human marginal
   distribution.** `human_like` recall stays ≤ 0.20 for every calibrated model. The remaining
   signal is temporal structure (drift, bursts, fatigue), which the placeholder humans have only
   weakly.
2. **Adaptive attacks overfit their target.** The round-0 optimum evades XGBoost (0.8%) yet is
   caught completely by One-Class SVM and by the original rule. This argues for combining
   heterogeneous detectors, consistent with the thesis's defence-in-depth stance.
3. **Adversarial training helps, but the game continues.** It recovers the round-0 attack
   (0.990), but a re-optimised attack still evades about half the windows (0.493). Human FPR at
   the 1% threshold changed from 1.4% to 1.7%, with overlapping CIs: no measurable cost here.
4. **The attacker pays in click rate.** Figure 3 shows the XGBoost utility frontier:

   | attacker's minimum CPS | 10 | 12 | 14 | 16 |
   |---|---|---|---|---|
   | recall, round 0 | 0.00 | 0.02 | 0.12 | 1.00 |
   | recall, round 1 | 0.22 | 0.55 | 0.96 | 1.00 |

   The detector does not stop automation; it caps the advantage automation can buy, which is
   the realistic "raise the cost" goal of the thesis.

![Figure 3](../ml/results/figures/adversarial_utility_frontier.png)
*Figure 3 (DEMO). Recall vs the minimum click rate imposed on the optimiser.*

### 5.3 Network lag (SIMULATED; DEMO and REAL timestamps)

Lag was simulated: per-packet jitter of σ = 10% of the ping, plus Poisson lag spikes that release
queued packets together. The ping → (σ, spike-rate) mapping is an assumption. Detectors keep
their clean-data thresholds and are evaluated on a fresh synthetic human population.

Realised human FPR at the 1% threshold:

| ping (ms) | 0 | 20 | 80 | 150 | 250 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| XGBoost | 0.028 | 0.024 | 0.076 | 0.123 | 0.026 |
| Random Forest | 0.026 | 0.025 | 0.021 | 0.026 | 0.009 |
| Isolation Forest | 0.009 | 0.028 | 0.149 | 0.398 | 0.540 |
| One-Class SVM | 0.013 | 0.079 | 1.000 | 1.000 | 1.000 |

- Even at ping 0 the tree models' realised FPR on this *new* population (2.6–2.8%) exceeds the
  1% calibration target: thresholds transfer imperfectly across populations.
- The novelty detectors are fragile under lag; tree models are less affected.
- Random Forest recall on attackers falls to 0.42 at 250 ms.
- **Real timestamps + simulated lag:** applying the same lag to P01's events breaks the
  held-button rule. Cleaned original-rule violations rise from 0 to 8.7 (ping 80) and 16.4
  (ping 150), averaged over 20 draws, because held swings get spread beyond 65 ms. The cleaning
  rule's robustness therefore has to be measured with real lag (protocol in `docs/real_lag_test_protocol.md`).

### 5.4 Explainability (DEMO model)

TreeSHAP on the exported XGBoost ranks:
1. histogram entropy (mean |SHAP| 3.89 log-odds);
2. mean interval (1.91);
3. lag-1 autocorrelation (1.13);
4. std (1.08);
5. p95 (1.00).

Permutation importance is near zero for all features. This is not a contradiction: the features
are redundant and PR-AUC is saturated, so permuting one is compensated by the others. Report
both, and prefer SHAP for per-decision explanations.

Each decision becomes an audit-log line. Example, a fixed clicker:

> `[ML-AutoClicker] FLAG (+1 VL) | score=1.000 threshold=0.964 (1% FPR on calibration humans;
> DEMO model) | window=50 swings, 48 active intervals, held_frac=0.00 | reasons: entropy=1.98
> (typical human 2.83) raises suspicion by 3.88 log-odds; mean=78.2 (typical human 149) …;
> ac1=-0.798 (typical human 0.002) …`

The third example in `ml/results/phase_g_results.md` is a **false positive on a synthetic
human**, driven by an extreme kurtosis (38.9) from one long interval. It shows why explanations
belong in the audit trail: a reviewer can see that the evidence is a single outlier, not
machine-like regularity.

## 6. Integration in the plugin

The extension follows the thesis's operational constraints.

- **Detector fix (all modes).**
  - The AutoClicker check now uses the arm swing as the click source, de-duplicated at 15 ms.
  - Held-button swings are excluded, with `LEFT_CLICK_BLOCK` and block breaks as dig markers.
  - Thresholds, VL scoring, RBAC bypass and asynchronous logging are unchanged.
- **ML engine.**
  - `ClickFeatureExtractor` (exact Java port) and `ClickModel` (ONNX Runtime, the only class
    that depends on it).
  - `MlClickDetector`: the main thread only appends swings. Every 25 swings a 50-swing snapshot
    goes to one background thread with a bounded queue, which drops and counts instead of
    blocking a tick. Resulting flags return to the main thread and feed `flagPlayer`.
  - The selected model is XGBoost: best recall at 1% FPR, and within 1 point of RF while being
    14× smaller (183 KB).
  - ONNX inference costs ~25 µs per window (median, Python onnxruntime on the development
    machine; not measured inside the server).
- **Fail-safe switch.**
  - `deteccion.autoclicker.engine: threshold | shadow | ml`, default `threshold`.
  - Invalid values, a missing ONNX Runtime or model, or a feature-order mismatch all fall back
    to the threshold detector with a warning.
  - `shadow` logs scores to `ml_shadow.csv` without adding VL, which is the intended way to
    validate on real players.
- **Privacy.**
  - The truncated UUID (`xxxxxxxx-XXXX-XXXX`), which keeps 32 bits of a public identifier, is
    replaced in `auditoria.log`, `telemetria_ml.csv` and `ml_shadow.csv` by
    `p-` + 64 bits of HMAC-SHA256(secret, UUID).
  - The key is generated with `SecureRandom` on first run and stored in the server's
    `config.yml`, never in the repository.
- **Verification.**
  - 11 JUnit tests: cleaner cases, Java↔Python feature and ONNX parity (max |Δ| 3e-7 in
    Python↔ONNX), the ML-thread hand-off, and the RFC 4231 HMAC test vector.
  - The jar builds with `gradlew build`. A manual smoke test on a local Paper 1.18.2 server
    confirmed that ONNX Runtime is fetched by the library loader, the model loads in `shadow`
    mode, the HMAC key is generated, and audit and telemetry lines carry `p-…` pseudonyms.

## 7. Limitations and ethics

**Limitations.**
- **Known issue from the manual server test.** On localhost, one held-button swing arrived
  67 ms after the previous one (a slow tick). The held rule treats any gap above 65 ms as a
  release, so the rest of that mining run was evaluated as clicking and raised AutoClicker
  violations. Separating the held tolerance (≤ 65 ms) from the release gap (e.g. > 150 ms) is
  the planned fix. In the same test the original Fly rule flagged a normal fall, and the
  original FastBreak rule flagged creative-mode breaking.
- **Evidence.** There is no real-data detection result: one participant, one 65 s session,
  probably in creative mode, and zero scoreable windows. Every detection number is DEMO and
  depends on assumed human parameters. The direction of some conclusions (e.g. the original
  rule's FPR) could change with real data.
- **Observation.** Tick quantization removes sub-tick timing. A client modified to send swing
  packets outside its tick loop could produce sub-tick timing the observation model does not
  include.
- **Evasion paths.**
  - Held-button routing can be abused by clicking on a block at tick rate.
  - Ping and TPS are not used as inputs, so detectors do not adapt to lag; the thesis's
    "conservative mode under poor network conditions" remains future work.
- **Attack model.** One optimiser with a small budget and one objective. Stronger attacks
  (gradient-free with larger budgets, or targeting an ensemble) were not run, so the adversarial
  results are optimistic for the defender.
- **Statistics.**
  - Windows overlap, and synthetic participants are i.i.d. draws from one hyper-distribution,
    so CIs do not capture between-population shift. Phase F shows the tree models' FPR rising
    from 1% to 2.6–2.8% on a new synthetic population even without lag.
  - 0.1% FPR is not resolvable with the current calibration sets.
- **Movement.** Movement ML (speed/fly) was not attempted: there are no labelled movement-cheat
  sessions.

**Ethics.**
- **Consent and data handling.** Recording players' input timing requires informed consent,
  minimisation and retention limits. The recorder stores no names or chat, participants are
  coded, and raw data are git-ignored. The new pseudonyms are keyed so that published logs
  cannot be linked to public UUIDs without the server's secret. This is still pseudonymisation,
  not anonymisation.
- **False positives.** A false positive is an unjust sanction. The integration therefore
  defaults to the original detector, recommends `shadow` mode until real validation exists, and
  attaches human-readable evidence to every ML decision.
- **Dual use.** The adversary generators produce only numeric interval sequences for offline
  evaluation; no input-injection or cheat tooling was written.

## 8. Future work

1. **Real data first.** At least 10 participants (≥ 30 for a 0.1% FPR operating point), each
   with combat, mining and exploration sessions in survival mode, plus labelled clicker
   sessions from several machines on the lab server.
   Re-run `run_all.ps1`; the real track activates automatically.
2. **Real lag.** Repeat Phase F with `tc netem` / clumsy, and measure the actual per-packet
   jitter from `moves.csv` inter-arrival times. Make the held-button tolerance adaptive if needed.
3. **Valid stress test.** Use headless test clients generating high-frequency events, measuring
   TPS/MSPT, recorder drops and ML-queue drops, to exercise the event-handling and
   asynchronous logging paths directly.
4. **Detector ensemble and VL policy.** Combine heterogeneous detectors (Section 5.2), and
   study how window flags should accumulate into VL under a session-level FPR budget.
5. **Movement models**, once `speed_mod` / `fly_mod` sessions exist (features are already
   implemented).

## References

- Alangari, A., Alharbi, O. (2025). *A Systematic Review of Technical Defenses Against
  Software-Based Cheating in Online Multiplayer Games.* arXiv:2512.21377.
- Breiman, L. (2001). Random Forests. *Machine Learning* 45, 5–32.

  Effectiveness of Client-Side Defences. *CheckMATE '24 (workshop co-located with ACM CCS 2024)*,

`docs/real_lag_test_protocol.md`:

```md
# Protocol: robustness tests under real network lag

Phase F (`ml/src/lag.py`) **simulates** lag by adding per-packet jitter and lag spikes to
recorded or synthetic timestamps. The mapping from ping class to jitter and spike rate
(`PING_CLASSES`) is an assumption. This protocol describes how to replace that assumption with
measurements taken on a real client–server setup.

## Hypotheses from the simulation

* Per-packet jitter of 8–25 ms is enough to break the held-button rule. Held swings are expected
  every 50 ± 15 ms, so with that jitter mining swings leak into the "active click" stream and the
  original 66 ms rule flags a legitimate player (`ml/results/phase_f_results.md`).
* Lag spikes release queued packets together, which looks like same-tick double clicks
  (`dup_frac`). Some models' FPR rises with ping (DEMO).

## Setup

1. Paper 1.18.2 test server with the plugin, using `deteccion.autoclicker.engine: shadow` so ML
   scores are logged to `ml_shadow.csv` but never add VL.
2. A client on a second machine, or on the same machine through a loopback shaper.
3. Traffic shaping:
   * **Linux server: `tc netem`** on the egress interface toward the client, e.g.
     `tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 40ms 8ms distribution normal` (RTT ≈ 80 ms with
     jitter). netem shapes egress only; for symmetric delay, shape both hosts or use an `ifb`
     device for ingress.
   * **Windows: clumsy**, filtering TCP port 25565 in both directions, using "Lag" for delay and
     "Throttle" for bursts. The settings used must be recorded with each session.
4. Conditions: target RTT of 20, 80, 150 and 250 ms, plus one bursty condition (throttling with a
   200–500 ms timeframe).

## Sessions per condition and participant

| block | label | duration |
|---|---|---|
| combat clicking | `human_combat` | 3 × 3 min |
| survival mining | `human_mining` | 2 min |
| reference clicker on the isolated lab server | `auto_fixed` / `auto_jitter` | 2 min each |

Sessions are recorded with `/ciberplugin record start <player> <label> <participant>`, in
survival mode, with the network condition encoded in the participant code (e.g. `P03_ping80`).

## Measurements

The recorder already stores the client-reported `ping` and a TPS estimate in `moves.csv`.

1. **Shaping check:** per-session median and IQR of `ping`.
2. **Jitter actually received:** the client sends movement every tick, so the spread of
   `moves.csv` inter-arrival times around 50 ms estimates the per-packet jitter seen by the
   server. The fitted values per condition replace `PING_CLASSES` in `ml/src/lag.py`.
3. **Held-button robustness:** fraction of swings classified as held in `human_mining` sessions,
   per condition. If it drops with ping, the held tolerance should become adaptive (e.g. 65 ms
   plus three times the measured jitter sd), in both `config.py` and `ClickStreamCleaner`.
4. **Detection under lag:** re-running `ml/run_all.ps1` reports FPR on real human windows,
   broken down per condition through the participant code.
5. **Server impact:** TPS with the ML engine off and in `shadow` mode, at each ping.

Real-lag results are reported separately from the simulated ones, with the number of
participants, sessions and windows per condition.

```

`gradlew`:

```
#!/bin/sh

#
# Copyright © 2015 the original authors.
#
# Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
# you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
# You may obtain a copy of the License at
#
#      https://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0
#
# Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
# distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
# WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
# See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
# limitations under the License.
#
# SPDX-License-Identifier: Apache-2.0
#

##############################################################################
#
#   Gradle start up script for POSIX generated by Gradle.
#
#   Important for running:
#
#   (1) You need a POSIX-compliant shell to run this script. If your /bin/sh is
#       noncompliant, but you have some other compliant shell such as ksh or
#       bash, then to run this script, type that shell name before the whole
#       command line, like:
#
#           ksh Gradle
#
#       Busybox and similar reduced shells will NOT work, because this script
#       requires all of these POSIX shell features:
#         * functions;
#         * expansions «$var», «${var}», «${var:-default}», «${var+SET}»,
#           «${var#prefix}», «${var%suffix}», and «$( cmd )»;
#         * compound commands having a testable exit status, especially «case»;
#         * various built-in commands including «command», «set», and «ulimit».
#
#   Important for patching:
#
#   (2) This script targets any POSIX shell, so it avoids extensions provided
#       by Bash, Ksh, etc; in particular arrays are avoided.
#
#       The "traditional" practice of packing multiple parameters into a
#       space-separated string is a well documented source of bugs and security
#       problems, so this is (mostly) avoided, by progressively accumulating
#       options in "$@", and eventually passing that to Java.
#
#       Where the inherited environment variables (DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS,
#       and GRADLE_OPTS) rely on word-splitting, this is performed explicitly;
#       see the in-line comments for details.
#
#       There are tweaks for specific operating systems such as AIX, CygWin,
#       Darwin, MinGW, and NonStop.
#
#   (3) This script is generated from the Groovy template
#       https://github.com/gradle/gradle/blob/HEAD/platforms/jvm/plugins-application/src/main/resources/org/gradle/api/internal/plugins/unixStartScript.txt
#       within the Gradle project.
#
#       You can find Gradle at https://github.com/gradle/gradle/.
#
##############################################################################

# Attempt to set APP_HOME

# Resolve links: $0 may be a link
app_path=$0

# Need this for daisy-chained symlinks.
while
    APP_HOME=${app_path%"${app_path##*/}"}  # leaves a trailing /; empty if no leading path
    [ -h "$app_path" ]
do
    ls=$( ls -ld "$app_path" )
    link=${ls#*' -> '}
    case $link in             #(
      /*)   app_path=$link ;; #(
      *)    app_path=$APP_HOME$link ;;
    esac
done

# This is normally unused
# shellcheck disable=SC2034
APP_BASE_NAME=${0##*/}
# Discard cd standard output in case $CDPATH is set (https://github.com/gradle/gradle/issues/25036)
APP_HOME=$( cd -P "${APP_HOME:-./}" > /dev/null && printf '%s\n' "$PWD" ) || exit

# Use the maximum available, or set MAX_FD != -1 to use that value.
MAX_FD=maximum

warn () {
    echo "$*"
} >&2

die () {
    echo
    echo "$*"
    echo
    exit 1
} >&2

# OS specific support (must be 'true' or 'false').
cygwin=false
msys=false
darwin=false
nonstop=false
case "$( uname )" in                #(
  CYGWIN* )         cygwin=true  ;; #(
  Darwin* )         darwin=true  ;; #(
  MSYS* | MINGW* )  msys=true    ;; #(
  NONSTOP* )        nonstop=true ;;
esac



# Determine the Java command to use to start the JVM.
if [ -n "$JAVA_HOME" ] ; then
    if [ -x "$JAVA_HOME/jre/sh/java" ] ; then
        # IBM's JDK on AIX uses strange locations for the executables
        JAVACMD=$JAVA_HOME/jre/sh/java
    else
        JAVACMD=$JAVA_HOME/bin/java
    fi
    if [ ! -x "$JAVACMD" ] ; then
        die "ERROR: JAVA_HOME is set to an invalid directory: $JAVA_HOME

Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the
location of your Java installation."
    fi
else
    JAVACMD=java
    if ! command -v java >/dev/null 2>&1
    then
        die "ERROR: JAVA_HOME is not set and no 'java' command could be found in your PATH.

Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the
location of your Java installation."
    fi
fi

# Increase the maximum file descriptors if we can.
if ! "$cygwin" && ! "$darwin" && ! "$nonstop" ; then
    case $MAX_FD in #(
      max*)
        # In POSIX sh, ulimit -H is undefined. That's why the result is checked to see if it worked.
        # shellcheck disable=SC2039,SC3045
        MAX_FD=$( ulimit -H -n ) ||
            warn "Could not query maximum file descriptor limit"
    esac
    case $MAX_FD in  #(
      '' | soft) :;; #(
      *)
        # In POSIX sh, ulimit -n is undefined. That's why the result is checked to see if it worked.
        # shellcheck disable=SC2039,SC3045
        ulimit -n "$MAX_FD" ||
            warn "Could not set maximum file descriptor limit to $MAX_FD"
    esac
fi

# Collect all arguments for the java command, stacking in reverse order:
#   * args from the command line
#   * the main class name
#   * -classpath
#   * -D...appname settings
#   * --module-path (only if needed)
#   * DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS, and GRADLE_OPTS environment variables.

# For Cygwin or MSYS, switch paths to Windows format before running java
if "$cygwin" || "$msys" ; then
    APP_HOME=$( cygpath --path --mixed "$APP_HOME" )

    JAVACMD=$( cygpath --unix "$JAVACMD" )

    # Now convert the arguments - kludge to limit ourselves to /bin/sh
    for arg do
        if
            case $arg in                                #(
              -*)   false ;;                            # don't mess with options #(
              /?*)  t=${arg#/} t=/${t%%/*}              # looks like a POSIX filepath
                    [ -e "$t" ] ;;                      #(
              *)    false ;;
            esac
        then
            arg=$( cygpath --path --ignore --mixed "$arg" )
        fi
        # Roll the args list around exactly as many times as the number of
        # args, so each arg winds up back in the position where it started, but
        # possibly modified.
        #
        # NB: a `for` loop captures its iteration list before it begins, so
        # changing the positional parameters here affects neither the number of
        # iterations, nor the values presented in `arg`.
        shift                   # remove old arg
        set -- "$@" "$arg"      # push replacement arg
    done
fi


# Add default JVM options here. You can also use JAVA_OPTS and GRADLE_OPTS to pass JVM options to this script.
DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS='"-Xmx64m" "-Xms64m"'

# Collect all arguments for the java command:
#   * DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS, and optsEnvironmentVar are not allowed to contain shell fragments,
#     and any embedded shellness will be escaped.
#   * For example: A user cannot expect ${Hostname} to be expanded, as it is an environment variable and will be
#     treated as '${Hostname}' itself on the command line.

set -- \
        "-Dorg.gradle.appname=$APP_BASE_NAME" \
        -jar "$APP_HOME/gradle/wrapper/gradle-wrapper.jar" \
        "$@"

# Stop when "xargs" is not available.
if ! command -v xargs >/dev/null 2>&1
then
    die "xargs is not available"
fi

# Use "xargs" to parse quoted args.
#
# With -n1 it outputs one arg per line, with the quotes and backslashes removed.
#
# In Bash we could simply go:
#
#   readarray ARGS < <( xargs -n1 <<<"$var" ) &&
#   set -- "${ARGS[@]}" "$@"
#
# but POSIX shell has neither arrays nor command substitution, so instead we
# post-process each arg (as a line of input to sed) to backslash-escape any
# character that might be a shell metacharacter, then use eval to reverse
# that process (while maintaining the separation between arguments), and wrap
# the whole thing up as a single "set" statement.
#
# This will of course break if any of these variables contains a newline or
# an unmatched quote.
#

eval "set -- $(
        printf '%s\n' "$DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS $JAVA_OPTS $GRADLE_OPTS" |
        xargs -n1 |
        sed ' s~[^-[:alnum:]+,./:=@_]~\\&~g; ' |
        tr '\n' ' '
    )" '"$@"'

exec "$JAVACMD" "$@"

:end
@rem End local scope for the variables with windows NT shell
if %ERRORLEVEL% equ 0 goto mainEnd

:fail
rem Set variable GRADLE_EXIT_CONSOLE if you need the _script_ return code instead of
rem the _cmd.exe /c_ return code!
set EXIT_CODE=%ERRORLEVEL%
if %EXIT_CODE% equ 0 set EXIT_CODE=1
if not ""=="%GRADLE_EXIT_CONSOLE%" exit %EXIT_CODE%
exit /b %EXIT_CODE%

:mainEnd
if "%OS%"=="Windows_NT" endlocal

:omega

`ml/README.md`:

```md
# ML extension: click-automation detection from server-observable timing

This extends the TFG's server-side anti-cheat (Minecraft Java, Paper 1.18.2) with a reproducible
ML pipeline. It keeps the same threat model (man-at-the-end), the same emphasis on false
positives, explainability, auditability and low server impact, and the same terminology
(VL, *bastionado*, fail-safe).

**Research question.** Can a statistical/ML model detect click automation (autoclickers with and
without jitter) from server-observable timing where fixed thresholds fail? How robust is it
against an adaptive adversary and against network lag?

> **Read this first: what is real and what is DEMO.** The real dataset is currently **one 65 s
> session from one participant (P01)**. It is enough for the data report, for the per-event
> behaviour of the original thesis rules, and for the timing-quantization observation. It is
> **not** enough for any detection metric: no real window survives cleaning (0 scoreable windows).
> Every recall/FPR/PR-AUC number below comes from **synthetic humans and synthetic adversaries**
> and is marked **DEMO - synthetic, not evidence about real players**.

## Reproduce

```powershell
# from the repository root
.\ml\run_all.ps1
# options: -RawDir <folder>  -WithCnn (installs PyTorch CPU for the 1D-CNN)  -SkipJava
```

What it does:
1. Creates `ml\.venv` (Python 3.12) and installs `requirements-lock.txt`, the exact lock (direct
   pins are in `requirements.txt`).
2. Runs the unit tests (29) and phases A–H.
3. Rebuilds the plugin with `gradlew build`, which runs 11 JUnit tests, including Java↔Python
   parity of the features and of the ONNX scores.

A full run takes ~5 min on a laptop CPU. A clean re-run reproduced every reported number (seeds
are fixed; differences appear only in the last digit of one bootstrap bound).

Steps one by one: `python -m src.data | src.synthetic | src.features | src.evaluate |
src.adversarial | src.lag | src.explain | src.export_onnx` (run from `ml\`, with `ML_RAW_DIR`
pointing at the raw folder).

## Layout

| path | content |
|---|---|
| `src/config.py` | constants shared with Java (marked `JAVA`), original thesis thresholds, seeds |
| `src/data.py` | **A** loading, schema validation, label normalisation, cleaning, quantization analysis, `results/data_report.md` |
| `src/observation.py` | client-tick quantization + network jitter model used by every generator |
| `src/synth_humans.py`, `src/adversary.py`, `src/synthetic.py` | **B** synthetic humans and adversaries (numeric sequences only) |
| `src/features.py` | **C** window features (50 clicks and 5 s, 50% overlap), movement features |
| `src/train.py`, `src/evaluate.py` | **D** detectors and participant-grouped evaluation |
| `src/adversarial.py` | **E** adversary levels, evolutionary attacker, adversarial training |
| `src/lag.py` | **F** simulated lag (real-lag protocol: `docs/real_lag_test_protocol.md`) |
| `src/explain.py` | **G** SHAP, permutation importance, audit-log explanations |
| `src/export_onnx.py` | **H** ONNX export, parity check, Java fixtures, latency |
| `results/` | figures, tables, `data_report.md`, `phase_[d-g]_results.md`, exported model |
| `data/raw/` | real sessions, **git-ignored, never commit** |
| `data/processed/` | derived data, git-ignored (contains real-derived data) |

## Data format

SessionRecorder (`/ciberplugin record start <player> <label> <participant>`) writes four CSVs per
participant folder (comma separator, dot decimals; `t_ns` = ns since session start):

- `clicks.csv`: `session_id,t_ns,source,label`, with `source` ∈ swing | interact_air | interact_block
- `moves.csv`: `session_id,t_ns,dx,dy,dz,on_ground,tps,ping,label`
- `breaks.csv`: `session_id,t_ns,label`
- `sessions.csv`: `session_id,participant,label,start_utc,end_utc,duration_s,events_dropped`

Labels: `human_combat`, `human_mining`, `human_explore`, `auto_fixed`, `auto_jitter`,
`speed_mod`, `fly_mod`; the legacy `human` maps to `human_unspecified`. Synthetic rows carry
`origin = synthetic` and are written as `data/processed/synthetic_*.csv`.

## Method in one page

**Cleaning.** These rules were derived from the real session and are identical in Python
(`data.clean_click_stream`) and Java (`ClickStreamCleaner`):
- **Click source:** only `swing` is a click.
- **De-duplication:** a swing within 15 ms of the last accepted swing is a duplicate. It is
  counted (`dup_frac`), not used as a click.
- **Held-button state machine:** `interact_block` or a break opens a dig. While consecutive
  swings stay ≤ 65 ms apart they are *held* (the client sends one swing per tick while the
  button is held). In the real data the break arrives *before* the held swings when blocks
  break instantly.
- **Moves:** trimmed 5 s after the **first move** (moves start ~12 s after `/record start`).

**Windows and features.**
- Windows of 50 accepted swings (stride 25) and of 5 s (stride 2.5 s).
- Statistics use the intervals of non-held swings, excluding pauses > 1 s.
- Features: mean, std, cv, skew, kurtosis, min, p05, p95, histogram entropy (25 ms bins),
  lag-1 autocorrelation, max clicks in 1 s, fraction of intervals within ±5 ms of a 50 ms
  multiple, duplicate fraction, pause fraction.
- Windows with ≥ 50% held swings are routed to "mining" and not scored.
- `held_frac`, ping and TPS are kept for audit but are not model inputs: as inputs they would
  teach "mining-like ⇒ human" and "high ping ⇒ …", which an attacker could exploit.

**Detectors.**
- Original thesis rule, fixed (flag if any interval satisfies 0 < diff < 66 ms, i.e.
  1000/15 with Java integer division), on raw interact events (`baseline_orig_raw`) and on
  cleaned intervals (`baseline_orig_clean`).
- A calibrated one-statistic rule (`cv_rule`).
- Isolation Forest and One-Class SVM trained on humans only.
- Random Forest, XGBoost, and an optional small 1D-CNN on interval sequences.

**Protocol.**
- StratifiedGroupKFold (5) by **participant**.
- Inside each training fold, 25% of the human participants are held out **only** to place the
  threshold at the target FPR. The test fold is never used for fitting or thresholds.
- Metrics: recall at FPR ≤ 0.1% and ≤ 1% (the realised test FPR is reported too), PR-AUC, F1
  and confusion counts at 1%, session alarms (≥ 3 flagged windows), and 95% bootstrap CIs
  resampling participants within class.
- With one real participant, leave-one-participant-out is impossible. The real track runs
  automatically when ≥ 2 groups per class exist, and falls back to session grouping with a
  printed limitation.

**Synthetic data.**
- *Humans:* a lognormal generator with AR(1) rhythm drift, bouts, pauses and fatigue. Its
  hyper-parameters are **placeholders** (`synth_humans.PLACEHOLDER_HYPER`: median 90–170 ms,
  log-sd 0.20–0.45, …), because the real data have 15 active intervals and the fit needs ≥ 300.
  It switches to fitted parameters automatically. Held-button mining sessions are included.
- *Adversaries:* `none` (fixed 8–20 CPS), `uniform` and `gaussian` jitter, `human_like` (i.i.d.
  draws from the human marginal) and `optimized`. The optimized level is a (μ, λ) evolution
  strategy that minimises a chosen detector's mean score while keeping ≥ 12 CPS within bouts.
- *Observation:* every generator goes through the same observation model. Client-tick
  quantization comes first, then per-packet jitter (σ = 2.5 ms, **calibrated from the real
  interval residuals**, n = 71), keeping TCP order.

## Results summary

### Real data (`results/data_report.md`)

- **One physical click = up to three events.** 34 of 110 raw click-event gaps are < 15 ms.
- **Original AutoClicker rule, per event, on the human session:**

  | stream | violations / intervals |
  |---|---|
  | naive interact events, as the Java code saw them | 1 / 33 |
  | all raw events | 59 / 110 |
  | swings, de-duplicated but **not** held-filtered | 56 / 76 |
  | cleaned (held excluded) | **0 / 20** |

  Switching to swings is only safe together with held-button exclusion.
- **Other original rules on legitimate play:** FastBreak 15 / 23 break intervals; Fly 7 (a
  fall); speed > 0.5 (effective clamped threshold) 70 / 608 moves, speed > 1.2 0 / 608. All
  breaks were instant (24 / 24), which suggests creative mode. Anecdotal: n = 1 session.
- **Timing is quantized to the 50 ms tick grid.** 73% of real *active* click intervals lie
  within ±5 ms of a 50 ms multiple, against 20% without quantization (n = 15, KS p = 0.014).
  See ![quantization](results/figures/real_tick_quantization.png).
- **Simulated lag on the real timestamps** (`phase_f_results.md`): jitter of ≥ 8 ms breaks the
  held-button rule. Cleaned-rule violations rise from 0 to 8.7 (ping 80) and 16.4 (ping 150) on
  average over 20 draws. This is a weakness of the cleaning rule that real lag tests must
  quantify.

### DEMO - synthetic, not evidence about real players

Phase D (50-click windows, 4,406 human / 16,902 attack windows, 40 + 72 participants):

| detector | recall @ FPR 1% | realised FPR | recall @ FPR 0.1% | PR-AUC |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| original rule (fixed) | 0.952 | **0.859** | – | – |
| cv_rule | 0.286 | 0.014 | 0.195 | 0.978 |
| Isolation Forest | 0.741 | 0.022 | 0.360 | 0.995 |
| One-Class SVM | 0.916 | 0.016 | 0.607 | 0.997 |
| Random Forest | 0.986 | 0.009 | 0.700 | 1.000 |
| XGBoost | 0.984 | 0.008 | 0.921 | 1.000 |
| 1D-CNN | 0.834 | 0.020 | 0.764 | 0.993 |

Points to keep in mind:
- The original rule's 86% FPR on *synthetic* humans comes from the placeholder's assumed lower
  tail (true intervals < ~75 ms are observed as 50 ms). It says nothing about real players.
- The 0.1% operating point is at the resolution limit of ~950 calibration windows.
- 95% CIs are in `results/phase_d_results.md`.

Phase E (adversarial; recall at 1% FPR, XGBoost):

| | none | uniform | gaussian | human_like | optimized | optimized_r2 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| trained on humans + auto_fixed | 1.000 | 0.908 | 0.960 | 0.059 | 0.008 | – |
| adversarial training | 0.998 | 0.956 | 0.978 | 0.197 | 0.990 | **0.493** |

- An adversary that resamples the human marginal (`human_like`) is nearly invisible to
  timing-only models.
- An adversary optimised against XGBoost evades it, yet is caught 100% by One-Class SVM and by
  the fixed rule: it overfits the targeted model.
- After adversarial training, a *re-optimised* adversary still evades about half of the windows.
- Human FPR at the 1% threshold moved from 1.4% to 1.7% (overlapping CIs).
- Utility frontier: forcing the attacker to ≥ 14 CPS gives recall 0.96 after adversarial
  training; at 10 CPS, 0.22.

See ![adversarial](results/figures/adversarial_recall_vs_level.png).

Phase F (simulated lag, fresh synthetic humans): XGBoost FPR goes 2.8% → 7.6% → 12.3% at ping
0 / 80 / 150 ms. RF stays ≤ 2.6% but loses recall at 250 ms (0.42). One-Class SVM reaches 100%
FPR from 80 ms.

Phase G: SHAP ranks histogram entropy, mean interval and lag-1 autocorrelation first.
Permutation importance is ~0 because the features are redundant and PR-AUC is saturated.
`phase_g_results.md` shows three decisions as audit-log lines, including a false positive on a
human.

**Plugin.**
- The XGBoost model is selected by the documented rule: best recall at 1% FPR, and if within 1
  point, the smaller model. 183 KB vs 2.5 MB for RF.
- It ships as `models/click_model.onnx` with Python/ONNX parity of 3e-7.
- Inference is ~25 µs per window (Python onnxruntime on this machine; not measured inside the
  server).
- It runs on a background thread, and the default `engine: threshold` keeps it off. Use
  `shadow` to log scores without VL.

## Limitations

- **No real-data detection result yet**: one participant, one 65 s session, probably creative
  mode, 0 scoreable windows.
- Synthetic humans use **assumed** parameters. Every DEMO number depends on them, especially
  the fixed rule's FPR and the separability of `human_like`.
- **Timing quantization** (client 20 Hz tick, a few ms of jitter) removes most sub-tick
  information. What remains is the distribution and sequence of tick counts.
- The **held-button exclusion is an evasion path**: an autoclicker hitting a block at tick rate
  is routed to "mining". It is also fragile under jitter (Phase F). Both need real-data checks.
- Lag robustness is **simulated**, with an assumed ping→jitter mapping.
- One optimizer (ES, small budget) and one objective (mean XGBoost score). Stronger adaptive
  attacks are possible, so recall against `optimized*` is an upper bound on robustness, not a
  guarantee.
- Movement ML (speed/fly) was not attempted: there are no labelled cheat sessions, and the 13
  real movement windows are only summarised (`tables/real_movement_window_summary.csv`).

See also `docs/ml_report.md` (paper-style report) and `docs/real_lag_test_protocol.md`.

## Design decisions

- **Raw data location** is configurable (`ML_RAW_DIR` / `-RawDir`), so the pipeline can run
  against data stored outside the repository.
- **Held swings arrive every ~50 ms** in the real data (one swing per client tick), so the held
  rule uses a 65 ms tolerance.
- **Feature definitions** use biased moments and explicit percentiles so that the Java port
  matches them exactly.
- **`held_frac`, ping and TPS are not model inputs** (see Method).
- **The plugin's default engine is `threshold`**, because the shipped model is a DEMO trained on
  synthetic data.

|---|---|---|---|---|

## Counts per participant

| participant | sessions | labels | duration_s | events_dropped |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P01 | 1 | human_unspecified | 65 | 0 |

## Click events per source (raw)

| source | events |
|---|---|
| interact_air | 10 |
| interact_block | 24 |
| swing | 77 |

Raw click events closer than 15 ms to the previous event (any source): **34** of 110 gaps (one physical click -> interact_block/interact_air/swing).

Breaks arriving <= 5 ms after an interact_block (instant breaks): **24 of 24**. Many instant breaks suggest creative mode or instant-mine blocks; the recorder does not store the game mode.

## Issues found during validation

- none

## Timing quantization (accepted swings, intervals < 1 s)

`all_*` = held + active swings (held swings are one per client tick by construction);
`active_*` = non-held swings only, i.e. real clicks. Only the active numbers speak
about click timing; with few active intervals they are anecdotal.

- all_n: 71
- all_frac_within_5ms: 0.7465
- all_frac_within_5ms_if_uniform: 0.2
- all_residual_mean_ms: -0.08708
- all_residual_sd_ms: 3.989
- all_residual_mad_ms: 2.416
- all_ks_vs_uniform_stat: 0.3566
- all_ks_vs_uniform_p: 1.383e-08
- active_n: 15
- active_frac_within_5ms: 0.7333
- active_frac_within_5ms_if_uniform: 0.2
- active_residual_mean_ms: -0.4943
- active_residual_sd_ms: 4.304
- active_residual_mad_ms: 2.41
- active_ks_vs_uniform_stat: 0.3921
- active_ks_vs_uniform_p: 0.01371

Interpretation: if the server saw continuous time, the distance of each interval to the
nearest multiple of 50 ms would be ~uniform on (-25, 25] ms and only 20% of intervals would fall within +-5 ms.
See `figures/real_tick_quantization.png`.

## Original thesis thresholds applied per event (REAL data)

Columns: `naive_java_interact` = consecutive LEFT_CLICK_AIR/BLOCK events exactly as
`onPlayerInteract` sees them; `naive_all_events` = every click event without de-duplication;
`dedup_swing` = swings after 15 ms de-duplication; `cleaned_active` = de-duplicated swings
excluding held-button (mining) swings. Click flag: 0 < diff < 66 ms (1000/15, integer
division as in Annex I). FastBreak: break interval < 450 ms. Speed: horizontal
displacement per move event > 1.2 (thesis text) or > 0.5 (effective value: config.yml
ships 0.1, clamped to 0.5 by loadConfigValues). Fly: not on ground and |dy| > 1.0.
Moves are reported with and without the 5 s spawn trim.

| metric | 0 |
|---|---|
| session_id | ea738255 |
| participant | P01 |
| label | human_unspecified |
| naive_java_interact_n | 33 |
| naive_java_interact_flags | 1 |
| naive_all_events_n | 110 |
| naive_all_events_flags | 59 |
| dedup_swing_n | 76 |
| dedup_swing_flags | 56 |
| cleaned_active_n | 20 |
| cleaned_active_flags | 0 |
| breaks_intervals_n | 23 |
| fastbreak_flags | 15 |
| moves_untrimmed_n | 649 |
| speed1.2_flags_untrimmed | 0 |
| speed0.5_flags_untrimmed | 70 |
| fly_flags_untrimmed | 7 |
| moves_trimmed_n | 608 |
| speed1.2_flags | 0 |
| speed0.5_flags | 70 |
| fly_flags | 7 |

All sessions are labelled human: every flag above is a false positive of the
corresponding rule on legitimate play (tiny sample: anecdotal, not a rate estimate).

`ml/results/phase_e_results.md`:

```md
# Phase E - adversarial experiment (DEMO - synthetic, not evidence about real players)

Optimized adversary: (mu, lambda) evolution strategy, minimum 12 CPS within bouts, objective = mean XGBoost score of its windows.

- vs round-0 XGBoost: median 40.0 ms, spread 0.763, rho -0.30, pause_prob 0.248; achieved 12.5 CPS, mean score 0.000
- vs round-1 XGBoost (optimized_r2): median 71.1 ms, spread 0.303, rho 0.58, pause_prob 0.029; achieved 12.1 CPS, mean score 0.737

## Round 0 - recall at 1% FPR per level

| model | recall@0.01[none] | recall@0.01[uniform] | recall@0.01[gaussian] | recall@0.01[human_like] | recall@0.01[optimized] |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| baseline_orig_raw | 0.890 [0.758, 0.975] | 0.999 [0.996, 1.000] | 0.966 [0.903, 1.000] | 0.824 [0.705, 0.913] | 1.000 [1.000, 1.000] |
| baseline_orig_clean | 0.890 [0.772, 0.974] | 0.998 [0.995, 1.000] | 0.966 [0.900, 1.000] | 0.824 [0.709, 0.919] | 1.000 [1.000, 1.000] |

# Phase G - explainability (DEMO - synthetic, not evidence about real players)

Model: final XGBoost (trained on synthetic humans + none/uniform/gaussian). Explanation method: shap 0.46.0 TreeExplainer; base value 2.120 log-odds. Evaluated on a fresh synthetic population (1837 human / 5488 attack windows).

## Feature ranking

| feature | description | mean abs SHAP | permutation importance (PR-AUC drop) |
|---|---|---|---|
| entropy | interval histogram entropy (bits) | 3.887 | 0.0032 ± 0.0002 |
| mean | mean click interval (ms) | 1.905 | 0.0004 ± 0.0001 |
| ac1 | lag-1 autocorrelation | 1.132 | 0.0002 ± 0.0000 |
| std | std of intervals (ms) | 1.079 | 0.0001 ± 0.0000 |
| p95 | 95th percentile interval (ms) | 0.996 | 0.0001 ± 0.0000 |
| kurt | excess kurtosis | 0.781 | 0.0002 ± 0.0000 |
| cv | coefficient of variation | 0.534 | 0.0000 ± 0.0000 |
| p05 | 5th percentile interval (ms) | 0.440 | 0.0000 ± 0.0000 |
| skew | skewness | 0.431 | 0.0001 ± 0.0000 |
| min | shortest interval (ms) | 0.403 | 0.0000 ± 0.0000 |
| max_cps_1s | max clicks in any 1 s | 0.310 | 0.0001 ± 0.0000 |
| pause_frac | fraction of pauses > 1 s | 0.272 | 0.0002 ± 0.0000 |
| tick_frac | fraction of intervals on the 50 ms grid | 0.133 | -0.0000 ± 0.0000 |
| dup_frac | fraction of same-tick duplicate swings | 0.049 | 0.0000 ± 0.0000 |

## Three example decisions as audit-log lines

Each line is what `auditoria.log` would contain (actor pseudonymised by HMAC in the plugin).

**attack window flagged with high confidence** (true level: none)

```
[ML-AutoClicker] FLAG (+1 VL) | score=1.000 threshold=0.964 (1% FPR on calibration humans; DEMO model) | window=50 swings, 48 active intervals, held_frac=0.00 | reasons: entropy=1.98 (typical human 2.83) raises suspicion by 3.88 log-odds; mean=78.2 (typical human 149) raises suspicion by 2.49 log-odds; ac1=-0.798 (typical human 0.00218) raises suspicion by 1.98 log-odds
```

**attack window missed (below threshold)** (true level: gaussian)

```
[ML-AutoClicker] no action | score=0.699 threshold=0.964 (1% FPR on calibration humans; DEMO model) | window=50 swings, 49 active intervals, held_frac=0.00 | reasons: entropy=2.85 (typical human 2.83) lowers suspicion by 3.06 log-odds; skew=0.0599 (typical human 3.09) raises suspicion by 1.38 log-odds; max_cps_1s=10 (typical human 10) raises suspicion by 0.91 log-odds
```

**human window close to the threshold** (true level: human)

```
[ML-AutoClicker] FLAG (+1 VL) | score=0.986 threshold=0.964 (1% FPR on calibration humans; DEMO model) | window=50 swings, 49 active intervals, held_frac=0.00 | reasons: kurt=38.9 (typical human 10.7) raises suspicion by 2.25 log-odds; std=123 (typical human 102) lowers suspicion by 0.94 log-odds; p95=153 (typical human 252) raises suspicion by 0.79 log-odds
